       1 +# ADR-018: The UI is a research workspace that shows its eviden
         +ce ("Ink & Signal")                                            
       2 +                                                               
       3 +- **Status**: Accepted (Brain Notebook)                        
       4 +- **Date**: 2026-10                                            
       5 +- **Related**: [ADR-011](ADR-011-design-token-system.md) (token
         + contract, kept), [ADR-014](ADR-014-agentic-notebook-chat.md) (
         +agentic chat), [ADR-015](ADR-015-page-aware-ingestion.md) (page
         +s, outline, concepts), [frontend.md](../frontend.md)           
       6 +                                                               
       7 +## Context                                                     
       8 +                                                               
       9 +After the research agent, page-aware ingestion and the concept 
         +graph landed, the UI still looked like upstream's:             
      10 +three equal boxed columns (sources, notes, chat), a chat that r
         +ead like any chatbot, page citations that opened a             
      11 +modal, and none of what ingestion builds (page images, outlines
         +, summaries, concepts) visible anywhere. The product's         
      12 +differentiators were invisible, and the "Quiet Green" palette (
         +squared, green actions, teal AI voice) gave the agent          
      13 +no presence.                                                   
      14 +                                                               
      15 +## Decision                                                    
      16 +                                                               
      17 +**The notebook page is a three-pane research workspace (library
         + · conversation · evidence) and the visual language is         
      18 +"Ink & Signal": paper surfaces, ink actions, an iris voice for 
         +the agent, amber for evidence.**                               
      19 +                                                               
      20 +- **Workspace**: a collapsible *library* (Sources · Notes · Con
         +cepts tabs), the *conversation* as the centred hero,           
      21 +  and an *evidence* panel that opens beside it when a citation 
         +or concept is clicked (rendered page with range                
      22 +  browsing and its outline section, or a concept with relations
         + and mentions). On narrower desktops the library               
      23 +  yields to the evidence panel; on phones they become tabs and 
         +an overlay. State lives in                                     
      24 +  `lib/stores/workspace-store.ts` (only layout preferences pers
         +ist).                                                          
      25 +- **Answers show their work**: a live research timeline while t
         +he agent runs (tool icons, arguments, result                   
      26 +  summaries, elapsed time, a rotating iris edge), folded into o
         +ne line afterwards; citations are amber chips whose            
      27 +  hover shows the cited page; every answer ends with a strip of
         + cited-page thumbnails.                                        
      28 +- **What ingestion built is visible**: page thumbnails on cards
         + and source rows, a *Structure* tab on every paged             
      29 +  source (metadata, summary, outline with page ranges, concepts
         +, all pages), notebook stats in the header, and                
      30 +  starter questions generated from the notebook's top concepts.
         + Backed by read-only endpoints                                 
      31 +  (`/notebooks/{id}/overview`, `/notebooks/{id}/concepts/{conce
         +pt}`, `/sources/{id}/structure`) and JPEG                      
      32 +  thumbnails (`/pages/{n}/image?format=jpeg`).                 
      33 +- **Tokens**: the ADR-011 contract stays (layers, semantic util
         +ities, no raw palette classes, dark mode on the root);         
      34 +  the values and laws change. Legacy names are kept so componen
         +ts didn't need a sweep: `teal` = iris (agent),                 
      35 +  `gold` = amber (evidence), `fern` = emerald (included / ok). 
         +New aliases `iris`, `amber`, `surface-*`, `ink-*`,             
      36 +  `on-agent`. **Laws**: ink acts (primary buttons are ink) · ir
         +is is the agent's voice (live state, focus) · amber            
      37 +  is evidence (citations, pages) · emerald confirms · red only 
         +destroys · paper surfaces, hairlines and soft                  
      38 +  layered shadows · radii 6–18 px · Instrument Serif for titles
         +, Geist for interface, Geist Mono for data and                 
      39 +  addresses.                                                   
      40 +

  ⎿  Updated frontend/AGENTS.md (+1 -1)
      12  ## Hard rules
      13  
      14  - **i18n is mandatory**: every UI string goes through `t('secti
          on.key')` and the key must exist in **all locales** under `src/
          lib/locales/` (currently 14; en-US is the reference). Missing k
          eys fall back to en-US silently — keep locales in sync. Each no
          n-en-US locale ends with `satisfies TranslationShape` (type der
          ived from en-US), so a missing/extra key fails `tsc`; the parit
          y test (`src/lib/locales/index.test.ts`) checks the same at run
          time. Adding a whole language also touches `src/lib/utils/date-
          locale.ts`; `src/components/common/LanguageToggle.tsx` renders 
          the `languages` list, so it needs no edit (see the [i18n playbo
          ok](../docs/7-DEVELOPMENT/change-playbooks.md#adding-a-new-lang
          uage)).
      15 -- **Styling goes through design tokens** ([ADR-011](../docs/7-D
         -EVELOPMENT/decisions/ADR-011-design-token-system.md)): use sema
         -ntic utilities (`text-destructive`, `bg-warn-tint`, `text-teal`
         -, `text-type-video`, …) defined in `src/app/globals.css` — neve
         -r raw Tailwind palette classes (`text-red-600`, `bg-amber-50`) 
         -and never hand-written `dark:` color twins (tokens re-resolve p
         -er theme). The laws: fern acts, teal is the AI voice, red only 
         -destroys, warn is clay, color never washes a reading surface. `
         -/dev/design` (dev-only route) renders every token and primitive
         - in both themes — it's the visual reference, and screen-level P
         -Rs must not change it.                                         
      15 +- **Styling goes through design tokens** ([ADR-011](../docs/7-D
         +EVELOPMENT/decisions/ADR-011-design-token-system.md)): use sema
         +ntic utilities (`text-destructive`, `bg-warn-tint`, `text-teal`
         +, `text-type-video`, …) defined in `src/app/globals.css` — neve
         +r raw Tailwind palette classes (`text-red-600`, `bg-amber-50`) 
         +and never hand-written `dark:` color twins (tokens re-resolve p

         +ADR-018-research-workspace-ui.md)): ink acts (primary buttons),

         +(`amber`, legacy `gold`) is evidence, emerald (`fern`) confirms

         +ations, research timeline, evidence/concept panels) lives in `s
         +rc/components/brain/`. `/dev/design` (dev-only route) renders e
         +very token and primitive in both themes — it's the visual refer
         +ence, and screen-level PRs must not change it.                 
      16  - All requests go through `apiClient` (`src/lib/api/client.ts`)
          ; never create a second axios instance. Auth token is auto-adde
          d from localStorage key `auth-storage`.
      17  - Data fetching uses TanStack Query hooks in `src/lib/hooks/` w
          ith `QUERY_KEYS`; mutations invalidate caches and show toasts (
          sonner). Follow the existing hook shape.
      18  - FormData requests: nested objects/arrays must be `JSON.string
          ify`-ed before appending; the interceptor strips Content-Type s
          o the browser sets the multipart boundary — don't re-add it.

      24  ## [2.0.0] - 2026-10-07 — Brain Notebook
      25  
      26  First release as **Brain Notebook**, a fork of Open Notebook 1.
          15.0 rebuilt around a research agent: chat, Ask and source chat
           no longer paste documents into a prompt but research the noteb
          ook with tools (page-level search, grep, outlines, reading and 
          *looking at* pages, a concept graph, sub-agents, optional web s
          earch) and answer with page citations. PDFs are ingested page b
          y page with equation recovery, vision captions, outlines, summa
          ries and metadata. The app is also an MCP server. Deployment bu
          ilds this repository (the upstream images do not include any of
           it). Code identifiers (`open_notebook` package, `OPEN_NOTEBOOK
          _*` variables) keep their names so upstream changes can still b
          e merged.

⏺ I redesigned the frontend, and it's live at http://100.120.164.122:3000. It's
  pushed to main and passes all tests, lint and the production build. I checked
  it in light, dark and phone widths, and ran one real question end to end
  through the new chat.

  What changed
  - Look and feel: a new palette with a serif display font for titles, in light
    and dark. The agent's live state is shown in violet, and citations and cited
    pages in amber.
  - Notebook workspace: three panes instead of three equal boxes.
    - Left: the library, with Sources, Notes and Concepts tabs. Sources show
      page-one thumbnails, and Concepts lists all 509 concepts with a filter.
    - Middle: the conversation.
    - Right: an evidence panel. Clicking a citation shows the actual page, which
      you can page through, plus the outline section it belongs to. Clicking a
      concept shows its relations, every page it appears on, and an "Ask about
      this" button.
  - Answers:
    - While the agent works, a live timeline shows each step and what it found.
    - When it's done, the steps fold into one line ("Researched in 5 steps · 2
      documents").
    - Hovering a citation previews the cited page, and every answer ends with a
      row of thumbnails of the pages it cites.
  - Empty chat: shows the notebook's size (7 docs · 794 pages · 509 concepts),
    four starter questions built from its own concepts (trace an idea, find
    every mention, read a diagram, connect concepts) and clickable key concepts.
  - Other screens:
    - Home: a greeting, an ask-across-all-notebooks box, a "Jump back in" row,
      and notebook cards with real page covers.
    - Sidebar: a search box, your notebooks for one-click switching, and an icon
      rail on phones.
    - Ask: rebuilt to match the notebook chat.
    - Sources: a new Structure tab with the summary, outline, concepts and every
      page.
    - Settings: the Research agent settings are now tiles, one per feature.
  - Behaviour change: Enter now sends a message and Shift+Enter adds a new line.

  I added three small read-only API endpoints the UI needs (notebook overview,
  concept detail, document structure) and small JPEG page thumbnails (about 16
  KB each).

  Existing bugs I found and fixed
  - Notebook chat was broken on the deployed app: every message failed because
    the API required a field the UI no longer sends.
  - The UI didn't work from other machines: it told your browser to call the API
    on port 5055, which only listens on the PC itself. It now goes through the
    UI's own address.
  - Notebook links in a fresh browser bounced through the login page.

  One slip on my side: a commit accidentally included two file deletions, so one
  deploy failed to build its new frontend. I restored the files and redeployed
  within a few minutes, and the PC is healthy.

  I also added a decision record for the new design (ADR-018), and updated the
  user guide, developer docs and CHANGELOG. You still haven't said whether to
  delete the old remote branches; I've left them alone.

✻ Cooked for 3m 33s · done Wednesday 12:08 PM

❯ perfect. Where do I find concept graphs                                       

⏺ The concept graph lives in three places, none of them a page of its own:

  1. Notebook → library pane → Concepts tab. Open a notebook and click Concepts
     at the top of the left pane (it shows the count, e.g. "Concepts 509").
     Clicking 509 concepts in the notebook header also opens it. The list puts
     the concepts shared by the most documents first, and the green bar shows
     how many documents mention each one. Type in the box to filter.
  2. Clicking a concept opens it in the evidence panel on the right:
     - Relations: e.g. "ReLU → replaces → Sigmoid". Click a related concept to
       jump to it.
     - Where it appears: each document with page chips. Click a chip to see that
       page.
     - Ask about this: has the agent explain the concept with citations.
  3. The empty chat shows "Key concepts" chips that open the same panel. A
     source's Structure tab lists the concepts in that one document.

  The agent also uses the graph itself through its graph tool, which you'll see
  in the research steps.

  There's no visual node-and-edge diagram. At 509 concepts a full diagram turns
  into a hairball, so I chose a list plus a detail panel. If you want a picture,
  I could add a focused graph view: the selected concept in the middle with its
  related concepts around it, click to move outward.

    Tailscale.

⏺ I closed the tunnel and built a proper concept graph. It's live at
  http://100.120.164.122:3000: open your notebook and click Graph in the header,
  next to Chat.

  What you see
  - The course as a timeline: each lecture is a coloured column, Lecture 0 on
    the left to Lecture 6 on the right.
  - Placement: each concept sits between the lectures that mention it, so the
    ideas the whole course shares (ReLU, loss function, convolution) land in the
    middle. Ideas specific to one lecture stay in its column.
  - Size and colour: bigger dots are mentioned more often, and a dot's colour is
    the lecture that introduces it.
  - Links: curved coloured links are relations the slides state ("uses", "is a
    type of"). Faint straight lines join concepts that appear in the same
    section; Shared sections at the top right hides them.
  - The layout animates into place when it opens.

  Interacting
  - Hover a concept: it and its neighbours stay lit, everything else fades, and
    the relation names appear on the links. A card shows which lectures mention
    it.
  - Click a concept: it opens in the evidence panel on the right, with its
    relations, the pages it appears on and Ask about this.
  - Move around: drag nodes, scroll to zoom, and use ⤢ to fit everything back in
    view.
  - Find a concept… flies the view to it.
  - Introduced in (bottom left): click a lecture to show only its concepts.

  It shows the 400 most shared of the 509 concepts.

  One deploy hiccup along the way: the PC's older npm rejected the lockfile my
  newer local npm wrote, so the first build failed. The PC kept serving the
  previous version, so nothing went down; I regenerated the lockfile with npm 10
  and redeployed. I also checked the graph on the live server. The changes are
  pushed to main, with the docs and CHANGELOG updated.

✻ Churned for 6m 11s · done Wednesday 3:12 PM

❯ It would be cool if the graph was 3d but is that practical or useful          

⏺ It's practical to build, but it wouldn't be more useful. It would mostly look
  cooler.

  It's easy to build. 3d-force-graph, which uses three.js, could reuse the same
  data and the same click-to-open evidence panel. It's about a day of work, and
  the roughly 150 KB of extra code would only load when you open the 3D view.

  It would be harder to read, for four reasons:
  - Things hide behind other things. In 3D, nodes and labels cover each other,
    and you can never see the whole graph at once. You have to keep rotating to
    find out what's behind the front.
  - Depth is ambiguous. A node that looks close to another may just be in front
    of it. The 2D map's main idea, lectures left to right, gets lost once you
    rotate away from the front view.
  - Labels and clicks get worse. Text in 3D is either billboards that overlap or
    labels that shrink with distance. Small concepts become hard to hover and
    click.
  - You'd have to drive it. Orbiting, panning and zooming in 3D is fiddly
    compared with scroll-to-zoom on a map.

  Research on visualising abstract networks says the same: 3D rarely helps
  unless the data has real spatial meaning, and a concept graph doesn't.

  Where 3D could earn its place is with a meaningful third axis, for example
  stacking lectures as layers along depth, with relation links running between
  layers. That's a striking way to see which ideas a later lecture builds on,
  but it still reads worse than the 2D columns once you're actually studying.

  My recommendation: keep 2D as the working view. If you want the wow factor, I
  can add a 3D toggle as a showcase mode. It would slowly auto-rotate, light up
  a concept's neighbourhood on click and open the same evidence panel. It'd also
  make a great README GIF.

✻ Sautéed for 12s · done Wednesday 3:13 PM
❯ ya go ahead and add itr                                                       

⏺ I'm adding a 3D showcase mode to the graph: a 2D/3D toggle, a slowly orbiting
  camera, lectures laid out the same left to right, glowing nodes, and particles
  flowing along a focused concept's relations. It reuses the same click-to-open
  evidence panel and loads its 3D code only when you switch to it.

⏺ The 3D graph is live at http://100.120.164.122:3000. Open a notebook, click
  Graph in the header, then 3D in the switch at the top centre (next to Map). I
  checked it on the live server.

  What it does
  - The scene: the same graph and the same left-to-right lecture order as the
    map. Nodes are spheres coloured by lecture; in the dark theme they get a
    soft glow.
  - Motion: it orbits slowly until you take over. Drag to rotate, scroll to
    zoom, right-drag to pan. Orbit at the top right restarts the rotation.
  - Focus: hover a concept, or click it to fly the camera there. Its
    neighbourhood stays lit, everything else fades, and particles flow along its
    relation links, coloured by lecture.
  - Evidence panel: clicking a concept also opens it on the right, with its
    relations, mentions and Ask about this.
  - Search and filters: Find a concept… flies the camera to a concept. The
    Introduced in legend filters by lecture, and Shared sections adds the faint
    co-occurrence links.

  Labels are HTML laid over the scene, so they stay sharp and the glow can't
  blur them; my first version, with labels drawn inside the 3D scene, was
  unreadable under the glow.

  The 3D code (about 150 KB compressed) only downloads when you first click 3D,
  so the rest of the app doesn't load any slower.

  The README now shows the 3D view under the graph screenshots, and the user
  guide, developer docs and CHANGELOG are updated.

✻ Baked for 9m 47s · done Wednesday 3:23 PM

❯ Sweet, what sets us apart from the market. What features do we offer that     
  other products don't offer (ex. notebooklm) and what do we lack.              

⏺ Web Search("NotebookLM new features 2026")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 17s

⏺ Web Search("NotebookLM vs alternatives 2026 deep research citations page
            images source limits")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 8s

⏺ You're unusually strong on research depth and transparency, and weaker on
  content generation, polish and anything social or zero-setup. My view of
  competitors comes from my own knowledge plus a quick web search, so details on
  fast-moving products like NotebookLM may be a few months stale.

  What sets us apart

  1. The agent investigates instead of retrieving. NotebookLM and most
  alternatives answer from passages they retrieve. Our agent decides how to
  research each question: it lists documents, greps, searches, reads page
  ranges, looks at pages and sends sub-agents per document, and you watch it
  happen.
  - That's why questions like "every page that mentions X" or "compare lectures
    3–6" work. Retrieval can't enumerate; grep can.
  - On the 35-question course eval: 35/35 correct against 27/35 for upstream's
    classic chat.

  2. It reads diagrams. When an answer is in a figure, the agent looks at the
  rendered slide. Visual page search finds "the slide with the inception
  module", and you can paste a screenshot to find matching slides. Most tools
  only work from extracted text and captions.

  3. Citations to the exact page. Every claim points to a specific page: hover
  shows the slide, and a click opens it beside the answer. NotebookLM cites text
  passages; we show the actual page, including figures and equations.

  4. Structure built at ingestion.
  - Recovery: equations hidden in LaTeXiT images become LaTeX again, and
    animation builds are merged.
  - Analysis: each document gets metadata (course, lecture number) and an
    outline with page ranges, so "the 4th lecture" or "section 3" resolve
    correctly.
  - Visibility: the Structure tab shows all of it.

  5. A persistent concept graph. It spans all your documents, with typed
  relations ("uses", "is a type of") and page-level mentions, and you can
  explore it as a map or in 3D. NotebookLM's Mind Map is a summary tree
  generated per notebook, not a navigable cross-document graph.

  6. You own it.
  - Self-hosted: no 50- or 300-source cap, and no lock-in.
  - Any model: about $0.003 per question with the two-model split.
  - Open and measured: you can change anything, and an eval harness tests every
    change.

  7. Other agents can use it. The MCP server lets Claude Code or Claude Desktop
  query your notebooks, including page images. NotebookLM has nothing like this.

  8. Grounding, memory and web are under your control. Each notebook can be set
  to "notebook only" (it says when something isn't covered) or to add general
  knowledge, with free self-hosted web search. Memory across conversations is
  controlled by you; NotebookLM's cross-notebook "Personal Preferences" is still
  being prepared.

  What we lack

  1. Study and creative outputs: the biggest gap for a student. NotebookLM's
  Studio produces audio overviews, video overviews (now "Cinematic"), slide
  decks you can revise, mind maps, study guides, briefing docs and FAQs in one
  click. We have upstream's podcast generator and free-form transformations,
  which I haven't tested in this fork, and no quizzes, flashcards or study
  guides.

  2. Speed. Our answers take 10–60 seconds, against a few seconds for
  NotebookLM. That's the cost of researching instead of retrieving; Quick mode
  helps but doesn't close the gap.

  3. Zero setup and reliability. NotebookLM needs no setup, has mobile apps and
  runs on Google's infrastructure. We depend on your PC being on, Tailscale, API
  keys and a deploy script.

  4. Sharing and collaboration. We're single-user, with basic password auth and
  no shared notebooks, public links or comments. NotebookLM shares notebooks and
  integrates with Google Classroom.

  5. Source breadth.
  - Connectors: no Google Drive, Docs or Slides integration (NotebookLM has
    these, and more connectors are coming).
  - Discovery: no feature that finds sources for you.
  - PowerPoint files: not yet ingested slide by slide.
  - Scans: scanned PDFs need OCR before they get page structure.

  6. Writing tools. There's no AI-assisted editor anchored to sources;
  NotebookLM is adding AI Editing and Canvas.

  7. Proven robustness. The eval covers one course with seven lecture decks.
  Papers, books and mixed libraries are untested.

  8. Paper-specific tools. Elicit and SciSpace extract tables across many papers
  and support systematic reviews, which a literature-review workflow needs.

  Where I'd invest next

  1. Study outputs built on our strengths: quizzes and flashcards generated from
     the outline, each linked to its page; "explain this slide" from the
     evidence panel; and a study guide that cites pages. This turns better
     research into the thing students actually use.
  2. A faster first response: stream a quick draft while deeper research
     continues.
  3. PowerPoint ingestion and OCR for scans, so lecture material in any format
     gets page structure.
  4. Read-only sharing of an answer: the answer with its cited pages, as a link.

  Items 1 and 3 are already on the VISION horizon.

  Sources:
  - NotebookLM: Canvas, Personal Preferences and Connectors coming in 2026
  - NotebookLM 2026-04 Update (BibiGPT)
  - Google Workspace Updates: new ways to customize and interact with your 
    content in NotebookLM
  - NotebookLM AI Editing and Personal Preferences rollout
  - Best NotebookLM alternatives in 2026 (Tana)
  - The 16 Best NotebookLM Alternatives in 2026 (Ponder)
  - Best NotebookLM Alternatives in 2026 (Recall)

⏺ I added a Market position (reviewed 2026-10) section to the root AGENTS.md,
  just before "Where to look", and pushed it to main.

  It has three parts:
  - What sets us apart: the eight differentiators, framed as things to protect
    when changing the code and to lead with in docs and UI.
  - What we lack: the eight gaps in priority order, starting with study outputs,
    latency and setup.
  - Next bets: study outputs that cite pages, a faster first response,
    PowerPoint and OCR ingestion, and read-only shared answers. It ends with a
    reminder that competitor details change fast.

  AGENTS.md is loaded into every coding session through CLAUDE.md, so future
  sessions on the repo will see it. I kept it to about 40 lines so it doesn't
  use much of each session's context.

         +-read them when the root `class` changes (theme switch) with a

         +s in the graph are evenly spaced hues (`documentHues`) so neigh
         +bouring documents never match.

  ⎿  Updated open_notebook/AGENTS.md (+3 -0)
      14  - Routers (`api/routers/`) call domain models and `repo_*` func
          tions directly. Logic goes in an `api/*_service.py` module only
           when it's shared or orchestrates jobs (`command_service`, `cre
          dentials_service`, `podcast_service`). New routers are register
          ed in `api/main.py` with `prefix="/api"`.
      15  - Provider metadata (env vars, modalities, test models, discove
          ry URLs, docs links) lives in the registry: `open_notebook/ai/p
          rovider_registry.py` `PROVIDERS`. `TEST_MODELS`, `PROVIDER_ENV_
          CONFIG`, `PROVIDER_MODALITIES` and `OPENAI_COMPAT_PROVIDERS` ar
          e derived from it, and `GET /api/providers` exposes it. Adding 
          a provider takes the registry entry **plus four hand-maintained
           copies**: the `SupportedProvider` Literal (`api/models.py`), `
          PROVIDER_CONFIG` (`open_notebook/ai/key_provider.py`), `env_var
          _map` in `get_provider_availability()` (`api/routers/models.py`
          ) and `PROVIDER_DISCOVERY_FUNCTIONS` (`open_notebook/ai/model_d
          iscovery.py`). Tests catch a missing Literal or discovery entry
          , not the other two. Follow the [Add an AI provider](../docs/7-
          DEVELOPMENT/change-playbooks.md#playbook-add-an-ai-provider) pl
          aybook. The frontend consumes `GET /api/providers` at runtime (
          `useProviders()`), so a simple API-key provider needs no fronte
          nd edit; the registry declaration order is the display order.
      16  - NEVER return API key values from any endpoint — metadata only
          .
      17 +- `api/routers/explore.py` is the UI's read-only view of what i
         +ngestion built: `/notebooks/{id}/overview` (counts, most shared
         + concepts), `/notebooks/{id}/concepts/{concept_id}` (mentions, 
         +relations), `/notebooks/{id}/graph` (top concepts with home doc
         +ument, relations, strongest co-occurrences) and `/sources/{id}/
         +structure` (metadata, outline, summary, concepts). It reuses `a
         +gent/retrieval.py` queries; keep it read-only and cheap (no LLM
         + calls).                                                       
      18 +- `/sources/{id}/pages/{n}/image` takes `max_side` and `format=
         +jpeg` (thumbnails, about 16 KB) besides PNG.                   
      19 +- `ExecuteChatRequest.context` is legacy and optional: the UI s
         +ends only `source_ids` / `note_ids`. Don't make new required re
         +quest fields the frontend doesn't send; a required `context` on
         +ce made every chat turn fail with 422.                         
      20  - Every user-supplied URL field must go through `validate_url()
          ` (`open_notebook/utils/url_validation.py`, async) for SSRF pro
          tection. Private IPs/localhost are intentionally allowed (self-
          hosted Ollama, LM Studio).
      21  - Errors: raise typed exceptions from `open_notebook.exceptions
          ` — global handlers map them to HTTP status codes (`NotFoundErr
          or`→404, `InvalidInputError`→400, `AuthenticationError`→401, `U
          nsupportedTypeException`→415, `RateLimitError`→429, `Configurat
          ionError`→422, `NetworkError`/`ExternalServiceError`→502, other
           `OpenNotebookError`→500). Don't raise bare `HTTPException` for
           domain errors. A router's catch-all `except Exception` (saniti
          zed 500) must come after `except OpenNotebookError: raise` so t
          yped errors reach the handlers (`tests/test_typed_exceptions_re
          ach_handlers.py`).
      22  - Requests over `OPEN_NOTEBOOK_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE_MB` (default 100
          ) are rejected by `MaxBodySizeMiddleware` before auth/routing.

          arget, questions queued from outside the composer via `ask()`) 
          lives in `useWorkspaceStore` (`lib/stores/workspace-store.ts`);
           only layout preferences persist. Open evidence with `openEvide
          nce({kind: 'pages' | 'concept', …})` rather than dialogs in the
           notebook; other screens (Ask, source chat) use `PagePreviewDia
          log`.
      18  - **Reuse `src/components/brain/`** instead of re-implementing:
           `PageThumb` / `usePageImage` (page images need the auth header
          , so they're fetched as blobs and cached for the session; thumb
          nails use `format=jpeg`), `AnswerContent` and `Citations` (cita
          tion chips, cited-page strip, `collectReferences` numbering), `
          ResearchTimeline` (`LiveResearch`, `ResearchTrace`), `EvidenceP
          anel`, `ConceptsPanel`, `ChatWelcome`, `SourceStructureView`, `
          ConceptGraph` (2D canvas, d3-force) and `ConceptGraph3D`. Read-
          only ingestion data comes from `lib/api/explore.ts` / `lib/hook
          s/use-explore.ts`.
      19  - **Heavy visual libraries load on demand**: three.js / 3d-forc
          e-graph only through `next/dynamic` (`ConceptGraphView`), never
           imported at module level by a page.

  Root AGENTS.md

    - How to run the UI locally against the PC's real data.
  - Hard rules (new):
    - Stage files explicitly, because git rm stages a deletion straight away
      (that's what broke the deploy earlier).
    - Write the frontend lockfile with npm 10, because the PC's npm ci rejects
      npm 11 lockfiles.
    - Refresh the README and doc screenshots when the UI changes visibly.
  - Where to look: adds the frontend architecture page and the screen-by-screen
    interface guide.

  frontend/AGENTS.md
  - Rules:
    - The notebook page's structure and its shared workspace state, including
      opening citations and concepts in the evidence panel.
    - Reuse the shared research components (page thumbnails, citations, research
      timeline, evidence and concept panels, both graphs) rather than
      rebuilding them.
    - Load the 3D libraries only on demand.
    - Translation keys must be written out literally, or the unused-key test
      fails.
  - Gotchas:
    - The canvas and 3D graphs read theme colours from CSS variables and re-read
      them on a theme switch.
    - The 3D graph has to remove its own stale labels on each refresh.
    - The login-bounce fix for deep links.
    - What API_URL=relative does.
    - Enter-to-send and container queries in the composer.
    - Mocking the notebooks list in sidebar tests.

  open_notebook/AGENTS.md
  - Explore endpoints: the four read-only endpoints the UI uses (overview,
    concept, graph, structure), which must stay cheap and never call an LLM.
  - Thumbnails: page images can be requested as small JPEG thumbnails.
  - Chat requests: the legacy context field is optional. Don't add new required
    request fields the UI doesn't send; that mistake is what broke chat with a
    422 on every message.

✻ Sautéed for 58s · done Wednesday 3:27 PM

❯ Does notebookLM have any competitors is there real value in turning this into 
  a production ready app. Because it could be very cheap to run using open      
  source models                                                                 

⏺ Web Search("AI study tool startups 2026 StudyFetch Knowt Turbolearn pricing
            students NotebookLM competitors funding")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 7s

⏺ Web Search("NotebookLM free plan limits 2026 NotebookLM Plus pricing
            students")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 6s

⏺ Short answer: yes, the market is crowded, and yes, there's real value, but not
  as a cheaper NotebookLM. Google sets the price at free (or $4.99), so cost
  can't be your edge. The value is a specialist: the best tool for studying
  slide-heavy, technical courses, with study outputs on top. Prove that cheaply
  before you build production infrastructure.

  The competition

  - NotebookLM is the anchor. It's free with 50 sources per notebook,
    $4.99/month for Google AI Plus, and has Google distribution and Workspace
    for Education deals. It just added more Studio outputs, and you can't
    undercut it on price.
  - Study apps are where students actually pay, and they're already big:
    - StudyFetch: about 7M users, raised $11.5M, $8–12/month.
    - Knowt: about 7M users, freemium, $150/year.
    - Turbo AI (formerly TurboLearn): about 5M users, $10–20/month.
    - Quizlet.

    They sell outcomes (flashcards, quizzes, study guides), not research.
  - General assistants: ChatGPT and Claude Projects (and study modes), and
    Perplexity Spaces. They're good enough for many students.
  - Research tools: Elicit, SciSpace and Consensus for papers.
  - Open source: upstream Open Notebook, AnythingLLM and Khoj, which cover
    self-hosting.

  The lesson: students will pay roughly $8–15/month, but for exam prep, not for
  an answer with citations.

  Where you could win

  Your strengths are precisely where these tools are weakest: technical course
  material, made of slides with diagrams and equations, spread over many
  lectures.
  - Exhaustive and cross-lecture questions: "every slide that mentions X", "how
    does Y evolve across lectures 3–6".
  - Answers that live in a diagram: "what does the residual block diagram show?"
  - Exact-slide citations you can check, with recovered equations.
  - A concept graph of the whole course.

  That's a believable wedge: "the study tool for STEM courses that actually
  reads your slides". But you haven't proven it against NotebookLM yet. The eval
  compared against upstream's chat, not NotebookLM, so that comparison is the
  first thing to measure.

  Cost with open models

  The research and answer models (Qwen 3.7 Flash, GLM 5.3 Flash) and the text
  embeddings are already open-weight, served cheaply through OpenRouter. My
  rough estimate for a heavy student:
  - Ingesting a course: about 15 decks of 100 pages at roughly $0.02–0.05 each
    (captions, outline, summaries, concepts, embeddings), so about $0.30–0.75
    per course once. Five courses is about $2–4 per semester.
  - Questions: about $0.003 each, so 300 a month is about $1.
  - Total: roughly $1–2 per active user per month at API prices. At $8–10/month
    that's a healthy margin.

  Some caveats:
  - Running your own GPUs only beats API prices at thousands of active users.
    Before that, idle GPUs cost more than per-token APIs. Keep using hosted open
    models.
  - Real costs shift elsewhere at scale: page rendering and PDF processing on
    CPU, storage, support and abuse. Free tiers need hard per-user caps.
  - Two closed models (Gemini page embeddings and Voyage rerank) cost fractions
    of a cent and have open alternatives.

  What "production-ready" would take

  - Accounts and isolation: multi-user auth, separate data per user, object
    storage for files, and secure defaults (today CORS is open and auth is a
    single password).
  - Scale: a scalable job queue for ingestion, per-user cost limits and rate
    limiting, and monitoring.
  - Speed: answers take 10–60 seconds today; stream a fast first draft.
  - Study outputs: quizzes, flashcards and study guides that cite pages. This is
    the thing people pay for.
  - Getting material in: importing from Canvas, Moodle or Drive, since lecture
    slides live in the course site. This alone could be a differentiator.
  - Legal: billing, privacy policy, and a clear stance on uploading course
    materials (slides belong to the professor or university, a risk every study
    app carries).
  - Mobile: at least a good mobile web experience.

  That's months of work for one person, mostly unrelated to what makes the
  product good.

  What I'd do before building any of that

  1. Measure head to head: run the same questions on the same decks in
     NotebookLM, on two or three courses beyond AI 360. If you don't clearly win
     on diagram, exhaustive and cross-lecture questions, stop here; you'd have
     learned that cheaply.
  2. Build quizzes and flashcards that link to the source page.
  3. Put it in front of 10–20 classmates for a few weeks. A small cloud VM with
     the current single-user setup per person is fine for a pilot. Track weekly
     use and whether they open it before exams.
  4. If they keep coming back, then build multi-user production infrastructure.

  Even if it never becomes a business, it's already worth something: an
  open-source, self-hosted tool with a measurable quality edge, an MCP server,
  and a strong portfolio piece. That path needs little beyond what exists now.

  Sources:
  - StudyFetch (Stork)
  - StudyFetch review 2026 (tl;dv)
  - StudyFetch vs Knowt (Stork)
  - Turbolearn alternatives (Mindomax)
  - Best AI study tools (Toolradar)
  - What you get with NotebookLM's free plan in 2026 (Elephas)
  - NotebookLM Free vs Plus vs Pro (FreeAcademy)
  - Expanded NotebookLM capabilities for Education Plus (Google Workspace 
    Updates)
  - Best NotebookLM alternatives 2026 (Tana)

✻ Brewed for 53s · done Wednesday 3:30 PM

❯ Me and claude could make this production ready in a week. I'm more worried    
  about serving something that nobody already has access to. By that I'm        
  talking about features. What could make this unique and provide actual value  
  which current apps don't already do. Maybe we isolate on a specific userbase, 
  students or researchers etc                                                   

⏺ I'd narrow to STEM university students in slide-heavy courses, and build
  features that only work because the system actually understands course
  material: pages, diagrams, equations and how concepts connect. Generic study
  apps can't copy those quickly, and NotebookLM's one-size Studio doesn't do
  them.

  Why students, and that slice

  - Proof: the eval shows exactly this advantage, on a real course.
  - Founder fit: you're the user, and your classmates are your first 20 users
    and your distribution.
  - Real gap: students already pay $8–20/month (StudyFetch, Knowt, Turbo), but
    those apps turn material into generic flashcards. None of them understands
    the course.

  Researchers are a viable second market (more below), but harder to reach and
  more crowded (Elicit, Scite, Consensus).

  Features nobody offers

  1. Exam map: "what's on the exam, and where is it taught?" Upload past exams,
  practice problems or the syllabus. The agent matches every exam question to
  the slides that teach it. It shows which topics are tested, which slides were
  never examined, and how heavily each lecture is weighted. Then it builds a
  study plan ordered by exam weight, every item linked to a slide.
  - Nobody does this, and it's exactly what students worry about before an exam.
  - It rests on what's already built: exhaustive grep and search across
    lectures, page-level addresses, and reading the problem images.

  2. Solve it the course's way. Paste a homework problem (a screenshot works).
  Instead of solving it like ChatGPT does, with its own method and notation, the
  agent finds the slides where this course teaches the method. It walks through
  the solution in the professor's notation, citing slides, and flags when the
  problem needs something the course hasn't covered.
  - Generic AI often confuses students because the method or notation doesn't
    match lecture. Following the course is the trust feature.
  - It rests on strict grounding, visual search for "slides that look like this
    problem", and recovered equations.

  3. "I'm lost": a path through the prerequisites. Say "I don't get backprop"
  and the concept graph traces what it builds on (chain rule → computational
  graph → Jacobian). You get a short path of 3–5 steps, each one slide with a
  one-line check question, ending at the concept you were stuck on.
  - Only possible with a cross-document graph of relations; nobody else has one.

  4. A formula sheet for the whole course. Every equation in the course,
  recovered as real LaTeX even when the slides render it as an image, grouped by
  concept with slide references. The agent can check it for completeness
  ("you're missing the bias-correction term from Lecture 4, p. 61").
  - Students make these by hand for every exam. Our equation recovery is the
    reason it works.

  5. Quizzes on the diagrams. Questions built from figures: "what does this
  arrow carry?", "label this block", "what changes if this skip connection is
  removed?", with the slide as the answer key.
  - Every study app makes text flashcards. In technical courses the hard
    material lives in diagrams, and we can actually read them.

  6. Your degree as one connected map. The concept graph spans semesters, so
  linear algebra from second year links to deep learning in fourth: "this was
  taught in MATH 221, Lecture 8".
  - Tools that work notebook by notebook can't do this. It also makes the
    product more useful the longer a student keeps it, which helps retention.

  What not to build

  Podcasts, generic flashcards, chat-with-PDF and summaries are commodities that
  NotebookLM gives away free. Keep what exists, but don't lead with it.

  If you go for researchers instead

  The strongest unique feature there is a citation checker for your draft.
  Upload the paper you're writing with its references; for each cited sentence,
  the agent checks whether the cited paper really supports it, with the exact
  page and figure. A results table built from figures (reading the plots, not
  just the text) and the MCP server for people writing in Claude Code add to it.

  How I'd start

  1. Build the exam map first. It's the most unique and most demo-able feature,
     and it's mostly orchestration on top of the agent we already have.
  2. Test it on a real AI 360 past exam or problem set: does every question map
     to the right slides?
  3. Show it to classmates before the next midterm. If they ask for it again
     before the final, you have your product; if not, try the course-method
     tutor next.

  I can start a prototype of the exam map now if you want: an endpoint that
  takes an exam PDF, maps each question to its slides, plus a view in the
  workspace.

❯ note down these features, so we shouldn't look to fill in gaps? that exist    
  between our app and notebookLM like pdcasts, flashcards, etc and should       
  instead focus on stuff that nobody has                                        

⏺ Mostly yes, with one distinction:

  - Don't chase commodities: podcasts, video overviews, generic flashcards, mind
    maps, summaries. NotebookLM gives these away free, and matching them wins
    nobody over.
  - Do clear the table stakes: being quick enough, reliable, easy to get
    material into, and usable on a phone. Users don't choose you for these, but
    they drop you if they're bad. Latency (10–60 seconds) is the main one we
    fail today.
  - Build differentiated versions where a category matters: students do want
    quizzes, so build diagram quizzes that link to the slide, not generic
    flashcards.

  AGENTS.md, and a decision note in memory.

❯ what file types are we able to ingest                                         

  Searched for 2 patterns, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Ingestion has two tiers. Only PDFs get the full treatment. Everything else is
  turned into plain text and searched as text.

  Full treatment: PDFs with a text layer (lecture decks and papers)
  - Pages are kept, so citations point to exact pages with previews.
  - Each page is rendered as an image, so the agent can look at diagrams and
    visual page search works.
  - It also gets page captions, LaTeXiT equation recovery, merged animation
    builds, metadata (course, sequence), an outline with page ranges, and
    concept-graph entries.

  Text only: everything else (extracted by upstream's content-core library)

  ┌────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Kind    │                          Formats                           │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Documents  │ DOC, DOCX, PPT, PPTX, XLS, XLSX, ODT, ODS, ODP, EPUB, TXT, │
  │            │  MD, HTML, HTM                                             │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Audio      │ MP3, WAV, M4A, AAC (needs a speech-to-text model)          │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Video      │ MP4, AVI, MOV, WMV (needs a speech-to-text model)          │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Images     │ JPG, PNG, TIFF (only with the Docling engine enabled)      │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Web        │ Article URLs and YouTube transcripts                       │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Pasted     │ HTML is converted to Markdown                              │
  │ text       │                                                            │
  └────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  These formats can be searched, read and cited, but they have no pages, no page
  images and no outline.

  Limits worth knowing
  - PPTX: no slide-by-slide pages. Exporting to PDF first gives much better
    results.
  - Scanned PDFs: with no text layer, they need Docling with OCR to get any
    text, and they still have no pages.
  - Upload size: 100 MB by default, set with OPEN_NOTEBOOK_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE_MB.

  Images pasted into the chat composer aren't ingested. They're used once, to
  find slides that look like the image.

  For the STEM-student focus, ingesting PPTX slide by slide is probably the most
  valuable gap to close, since many courses publish decks as PowerPoint.
  Converting PPTX to PDF on upload (for example with LibreOffice running
  headless) would give PPTX files the full treatment without a second pipeline.

  didn't.

  1. Slide numbers don't match PDF pages. NLP's builds are separate PDF pages,
     so the slide printed "29" is PDF page 60. Parallel Computation is similar
     (page 8 shows "7/14"). A student who asks about "slide 29" would get page
     29, and citations would show numbers that don't match the slides. The fix
     is to read the printed slide number on each page and use it alongside the
     page.
  2. Math in the LaTeX slides comes out garbled. On Parallel Computation pages,
     a formula like T_w + (N/P − 1)·T_c extracts as scraps on separate lines
     plus junk such as (cid:124)(cid:123). Captions only cover image-heavy
     pages, so none of these math pages get captioned, and text search would
     miss the formulas. The agent can still look at the rendered page, but the
     formula sheet feature from the plan depends on fixing this. The fix is to
     treat pages with that junk as visual and caption them, which recovers the
     formula.
  3. The Cloud Computing slides say "Do not redistribute". They're fine for your
     own notebook but shouldn't go into README screenshots.

  One thing works in our favour: the Cloud Computing slides include in-class
  prediction questions ("What if we double the cores?") whose answers come later
  in the deck. That's good test material for the exam map.

  fix the slide numbers and garbled math first, so they're ingested correctly
  the first time? I'd fix first: both are small changes to ingestion, and

⏺ I'd read the number printed on each slide from the PDF itself, with no model
  calls. Tested against all 29 decks, it got every printed number it could find
  right and never invented one where none is printed.

  How it works

  1. Use the PDF's own page labels when it has them. Some PDFs store page
     labels; LaTeX slides do this when they have builds. Two of your Parallel
     Computation decks have them, and they're authoritative. Most decks don't.
  2. Otherwise, find the slide-number position for the whole deck, not page by 
     page.
     - On every page, collect text near the top and bottom edges that is only a
       number (12, 7/14, 12 / 49), noting which corner it's in.
     - For each corner, check whether those numbers form a sequence across the
       deck: they must never go down, may repeat (builds) or skip one, and must
       appear on at least half the pages.
     - The best-fitting corner is the slide-number position. Checking the whole
       deck is what makes this reliable: a stray "3" in a chart axis or footnote
       won't fit a sequence, so it's ignored.
  3. Never guess.
     - A page without a number inherits one only if it's part of a build of a
       numbered slide.
     - Otherwise it gets no label.
     - Decks with no printed numbers get no labels at all.
  4. Keep PDF pages as the address. They're unique and stable, so citations and
     page images keep using them. The printed number is extra information:
     - "slide 29" resolves to every PDF page labelled 29 (all builds of that
       slide).
     - Citation chips show the slide number students see.
     - The agent's tools show both.

  Results on your decks

  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │       Course        │                      Result                       │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cloud Computing (8  │ Bottom-right corner, all pages, 1:1 with PDF      │
  │ decks)              │ pages                                             │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ NLP (7 decks)       │ Bottom-right; 738 PDF pages map to about 330      │
  │                     │ slides. Lecture 1: slide 29 = page 60             │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Parallel            │ Bottom-right (7/14 style); agrees exactly with    │
  │ Computation (7      │ the PDF labels on both decks that have them       │
  │ decks)              │                                                   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Deep Learning (7    │ No printed numbers: no labels, PDF pages as today │
  │ decks)              │                                                   │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  NLP lecture 1 only gets 72% of pages labelled. I rendered the gaps: those
  slides genuinely have no number printed, so leaving them unlabelled is right.

  Bonus and limits

  - Builds: pages sharing a printed number are the same slide. That's a stronger
    signal than the text comparison now used to merge animation builds, so the
    two checks can work together.
  - Not covered: numbers drawn as images or in scanned PDFs (they'd need the
    page images read by a model; not worth it yet), and numbers with a prefix
    like "Slide 12" or "p. 12" (a small pattern extension).
  - Papers and books: the same method gives printed page numbers there, and
    books usually carry PDF page labels anyway.

  Should I build this together with the math fix (captioning pages whose
  formulas extract as junk), and then ingest the three courses?

⏺ Agreed, slide numbers are dropped. Yes, one more thing needs fixing, and it
  also affects your existing Deep Learning notebook.

  Diagrams drawn as shapes are never described

  Ingestion only writes a description (caption) for pages that are at least 25%
  raster image (photos, screenshots). Many diagrams in your courses aren't
  raster images: PowerPoint and LaTeX draw them as lines and shapes. I rendered
  pages it skips:

  - Parallel Computation: the reduce tree on reduce-alg page 8. Its text layer
    is just "rank rank rank … N/P poems".
  - NLP: the RAG pipeline diagram (lecture 1, page 25).
  - Deep Learning: the layer-stack diagram (lecture 0, page 65).

  The agent can still look at these pages when it opens them. But search can't
  find them, so a question like "how does the reduce tree work" or "what does
  the RAG pipeline look like" has nothing to match.

  Counting slides that have a drawing beyond their deck's usual template shapes,
  but no description:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Course              │   Uncaptioned slides with drawings    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cloud Computing                 │ 43 of 267 (some are just tables)      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ NLP                             │ 71 of 303                             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Parallel Computation            │ 17 of 87 (plus 7 pages of garbled     │
  │                                 │ math)                                 │
  ├─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Deep Learning (already          │ 87 of 334                             │
  │ ingested)                       │                                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  The fix

  This also covers the garbled math from before, and it's one rule change. A
  page gets a description when any of these holds:

  1. It is at least 25% raster image (today's rule).
  2. It has noticeably more drawn shapes than the deck's usual page. Comparing
     against the deck's usual page means the shapes every page shares, like a
     LaTeX template's navigation icons, don't count.
  3. Its text came out garbled (the (cid:…) junk).

  The garbled scraps would also be stripped from the indexed text.

  The description prompt already handles all three cases: it writes out
  equations in LaTeX, transcribes flow diagrams as nodes and edges, and replies
  "no visual content" for false positives like plain tables, so those cost
  nothing to index.

  The cost is about 220 extra descriptions across all four courses, roughly
  $0.10. I'd also re-run descriptions for the Deep Learning notebook so it gets
  its 87 diagrams.

  Minor, not a code fix

  The Parallel Computation files carry no lecture numbers, either in their file
  names or on the slides. Their order in the concept graph and in "the 3rd
  lecture" questions would then be a guess. If you know the course order, I can
  set it, or renaming the files with a number prefix is enough.

  Should I make the fix, ingest the three courses, and refresh the Deep Learning
  descriptions?

✻ Cogitated for 6m 28s · done Wednesday 8:59 PM

❯ what is the prompt fix and why do you have to do so much post processing      
  work. This definitely isn't production ready                                  

⏺ The prompt fix

  The caption model is asked: "describe what the image adds beyond the extracted
  text; if nothing, reply NO_VISUAL_CONTENT". On a garbled-math page, the
  extracted text still holds scraps of the formula (T + −1 T +(P −1)T . w c c 
  P). The model saw something that looked like the formula and said the image
  added nothing. So the page was correctly selected for captioning, but no
  caption was saved.

  The fix is one sentence, added only for garbled pages: "the extracted text's
  math is garbled, so transcribe every equation in LaTeX." With it, the model
  wrote the formula correctly.

  Why there's so much cleanup

  You're right that this isn't production-ready. Part of the mess was my own
  process:
  - I didn't check the captions before ingesting. I verified that the right
    pages were selected, but not what the model wrote for them. Running it on
    the garbled page first would have caught the "no visual content" reply.
  - Tooling mistakes: the tar junk files, the SSH pager hangs and bugs in my
    timing script were my errors, not the app's.

  The other part is real gaps in the app, which this run exposed:
  1. Ingested data never gets updated when the pipeline improves. Every
     ingestion fix needs a hand-run backfill script (recaption_pages.py) for
     notebooks that already exist. Production would record which pipeline
     version processed each source, and reprocess outdated stages automatically
     after a deploy.
  2. Deploying kills running jobs. Restarting the worker leaves jobs marked
     "running" forever; I found 8 orphaned ones from earlier deploys. That's why
     I have to wait for ingestion to finish before deploying. Interrupted jobs
     should be put back in the queue on restart, so a deploy is safe at any
     time.
  3. The app doesn't track its own ingestion. I had to write an outside script
     to time stages and tell when a source is truly finished. The source should
     record when each stage finished and show progress in the UI.
  4. Ingestion changes aren't tested before they reach real notebooks. There's
     an answer eval, but nothing checks which pages get captioned and what the
     captions say. A small fixture set (a diagram page, a garbled-math page, a
     template-heavy deck) with expected outcomes would have caught this bug in
     CI.

  The first two would have made today routine: deploy the fix whenever, and
  existing notebooks update themselves. Once Cloud Computing and NLP finish,
  should I build those two, then add the caption fixture tests?

         + graph) changed.

      31 +  useEffect(() => {

      34 +      queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['explore'] }) 
      35 +    }                                                          
      36 +    wasComplete.current = complete                             
      37 +  }, [complete, queryClient])                                  
      38 +  return query

          +PEN_NOTEBOOK_AUTO_REPROCESS=false` to turn this off.

          +hing: the worker re-queues the interrupted jobs when it

          eys fall back to en-US silently — keep locales in sync. The unu
          sed-key test (same file) fails on keys no source file reference
          s **literally**, so never build keys from templates (``t(`x.${k
          }`)``); map to literal `t('x.a')` calls instead, and delete key
          s from all locales when their last use goes. Each non-en-US loc
          ale ends with `satisfies TranslationShape` (type derived from e
          n-US), so a missing/extra key fails `tsc`; the parity test (`sr
          c/lib/locales/index.test.ts`) checks the same at runtime. Addin
          g a whole language also touches `src/lib/utils/date-locale.ts`;
           `src/components/common/LanguageToggle.tsx` renders the `langua
          ges` list, so it needs no edit (see the [i18n playbook](../docs
          /7-DEVELOPMENT/change-playbooks.md#adding-a-new-language)).
      16  - **Styling goes through design tokens** ([ADR-011](../docs/7-D
          EVELOPMENT/decisions/ADR-011-design-token-system.md)): use sema
          ntic utilities (`text-destructive`, `bg-warn-tint`, `text-teal`
          , `text-type-video`, …) defined in `src/app/globals.css` — neve
          r raw Tailwind palette classes (`text-red-600`, `bg-amber-50`) 
          and never hand-written `dark:` color twins (tokens re-resolve p
          er theme). The laws ([ADR-018](../docs/7-DEVELOPMENT/decisions/
          ADR-018-research-workspace-ui.md)): ink acts (primary buttons),
           iris (`text-iris`, legacy `teal`) is the agent's voice, amber 
          (`amber`, legacy `gold`) is evidence, emerald (`fern`) confirms
          , red only destroys. `/dev/design` (dev-only route) renders eve
          ry token and primitive in both themes — it's the visual referen
          ce, and screen-level PRs must not change it.
      17  - **The notebook page is a workspace** ([ADR-018](../docs/7-DEV
          ELOPMENT/decisions/ADR-018-research-workspace-ui.md)): library 
          (Sources · Notes · Concepts) · Chat or Graph · evidence panel. 
          Cross-pane state (library open/tab, Chat/Graph view, evidence t
          arget, questions queued from outside the composer via `ask()`) 
          lives in `useWorkspaceStore` (`lib/stores/workspace-store.ts`);
           only layout preferences persist. Open evidence with `openEvide
          nce({kind: 'pages' | 'concept', …})` rather than dialogs in the
           notebook; other screens (Ask, source chat) use `PagePreviewDia
          log`.

      71  - Commands are `@command("<name>", app="open_notebook", retry={
          ...})` functions and must be imported from `commands/__init__.p

      72  - Retry config uses a blocklist: exceptions in `stop_on` (`Valu
          eError`, `ConfigurationError`, `NotFoundError`, … — check the c
          ommand's list) fail the job permanently (no retry, job marked `

      73 +- Submission is fire-and-forget via `submit_command()`; command
         +s must be idempotent: a job may run again after a worker restar
         +t (the worker re-queues interrupted jobs) or a reprocess.      
      74 +- **Ingestion stages are tracked** ([ADR-019](../docs/7-DEVELOP
         +MENT/decisions/ADR-019-tracked-versioned-ingestion.md)): a stag
         +e command runs inside `tracked(source_id, stage)` and records t
         +he next stage with `stage_queued()` beside its `submit_command(
         +)` (`open_notebook/domain/ingestion.py`). A change to a stage's
         + code or prompt that existing sources should get **bumps that s
         +tage in `STAGE_VERSIONS`** (with the reason in the comment); th
         +e next worker start reprocesses outdated sources from that stag
         +e. Don't write one-off backfill scripts.                       
      75 +- Prompt templates are cached per process: a prompt change reac
         +hes jobs only after the worker restarts (deploys restart it).  
      76  - Podcast generation uses `max_attempts: 1` on purpose (prevent
          s duplicate episodes); retry is the explicit `POST /api/podcast
          s/episodes/{id}/retry` endpoint.

      12  - **Concept graph view.** **Graph** in the notebook header (or 
          *Open the concept graph* in the Concepts tab) draws the noteboo
          k's concepts as a live map: one column per document in course o
          rder, each concept placed between the documents that mention it
           and coloured by the one that introduces it, stated relations a
          s curved links and shared sections as faint ones. Hover to focu
          s a concept and its neighbours (relation labels appear), click 
          to open it in the evidence panel, search to fly to one, drag, z
          oom, and filter by document from the legend. A **3D** mode (Web
          GL, loaded only when opened) shows the same graph as an orbitin
          g scene with glow in the dark theme and particles flowing along
           a focused concept's relations. API: `GET /api/notebooks/{id}/g
          raph`
      13  - `GET /api/notebooks/{id}/overview` (document, page, section, 
          note and concept counts plus the most shared concepts), `GET /a
          pi/notebooks/{id}/concepts/{concept_id}` (a concept's mentions 
          and relations) and `GET /api/sources/{id}/structure` (metadata,
           page count, summary, outline, concepts)

          urces/{id}/pages/{n}/image?max_side=480&format=jpeg`)
      15 -- `scripts/brain/recaption_pages.py --notebook NAME` captions t
         -he newly qualifying pages of PDFs ingested earlier (then re-emb
         -eds and re-analyzes those sources)                             
      15 +- **Ingestion progress.** Every processing step of a source (re
         +ading pages, describing visuals, indexing text, outlining, page
         + images, concepts) records its status, timing and errors. The S
         +ources library shows an indexing strip (documents ready, steps 
         +running) and each source its current or failed step with **Retr
         +y**; the Structure tab lists every step with its duration. API:
         + `GET /api/notebooks/{id}/ingestion`, `GET /api/sources/{id}/in
         +gestion`, `POST /api/sources/{id}/ingestion/retry`. `scripts/br
         +ain/ingest_folder.py --wait` follows a folder's ingestion and p
         +rints per-step timing ([ADR-019](docs/7-DEVELOPMENT/decisions/A
         +DR-019-tracked-versioned-ingestion.md))                        
      16 +- **Existing documents get pipeline improvements automatically.
         +** Steps carry versions; when the worker starts, sources proces
         +sed by an older version of a step are re-run from that step, re
         +doing only what changed (captions, insights and page images are
         + kept). `OPEN_NOTEBOOK_AUTO_REPROCESS=false` turns it off

          origin; `scripts/brain/install_services.sh` sets it, so a syste
          md install whose API listens on 127.0.0.1 works from other mach
          ines. Existing installs: add `Environment=API_URL=relative` to 
          `brain-frontend.service`

⏺ All 6 pass. Checking the deploy:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I found the deploy bug: deploy_pc.sh pulls a new version mid-run, but since
  bash already started executing the old copy, the upgrade step never actually
  took effect, leaving the worker to restart without recovery or reprocessing.
  The fix is to have the script re-exec itself after the pull so every deploy
  consistently runs the pulled version.

⏺ I found the bug: migrations 30/31 added required fields that SurrealDB only
  defaults on new records, so existing rows fail validation on update. I'll add
  migration 32 to backfill those fields and update worker startup recovery to
  also resume failed/stalled sources, not just outdated ones, so the fix
  self-heals affected data on deploy.

⏺ I'll tag each restart with a reason so I know how far to run the chain:
  outdated-version restarts only continue downstream if something changed, while
  restarts from a failure or stalled chain always continue since later stages
  never ran.

⏺ I've spotted two more issues: stuck "queued" stages left hanging when their
  job dies early, and retries that would needlessly re-run all downstream stages
  even if nothing changed. I'll fix both—treat orphaned queued/running stages
  as stalled, and only continue retries past completed stages if their output
  actually changed.

         +e (its job died, or failed before reaching it) counts          
      44 +  as pending. When the stages after the restart point already f
         +inished, the pipeline continues past it only if its            
      45 +  output changed. A                                            
      46    failed extraction of a source without pages needs its content
           re-read and is left to the user.
      47  - **Visible progress**: `GET /api/sources/{id}/ingestion`, `GET
           /api/notebooks/{id}/ingestion` and
      48    `POST /api/sources/{id}/ingestion/retry`; the library shows a
          n indexing strip and per-source stage, the Structure

⏺ Background command "Wait for full integration run" completed (exit code 0)

⏺ Found a bug: legacy sources ingested before tracking existed lack stage rows,
  so they're perpetually misread as pending and get restarted endlessly. Fix:
  treat sources without an extract row as legacy, backfill their already-done
  stages with real rows before restarting, then resume from the earliest
  failed/pending/outdated stage in pipeline order.

  and frontend simultaneously, letting the worker start processing jobs on the
  old schema before migrations finish. I'll change the deploy to restart the API
  first, wait for it to be fully up, then restart the worker and frontend.
